</span>            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="This adds `1.6875 bpw` and `2.0625 bpw` quant types for TriLMs and BitNet b1.58 models. For now, these are named `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0`, respectively.
I had given glimpses of this idea starting from &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/7931#discussion_r1640265346&gt;.

The `1.6875 bpw` type mostly relies on the fact that &lt;code&gt;3&lt;sup&gt;5&lt;/sup&gt; == 243 &lt; 256 == 2&lt;sup&gt;8&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/code&gt; to pack 5 trits per byte.

(I also made [a blog post about ternary packing](https://compilade.net/blog/ternary-packing) in an attempt to explain the core idea a bit more (storing the values in fixed-point to extract the most significant digit first with multiplications))

Huge thanks to @Eddie-Wang1120, who motivated this by adding initial BitNet b1.58 support in #7931.

## How to try it

Using [TriLM models](https://huggingface.co/collections/SpectraSuite/trilms-unpacked-668d5f62afe0f4036925b1d2) is the easiest because all of their models have row sizes divisible by 256.

&gt; [!IMPORTANT]
&gt; To quantize the token embeddings and the output tensor to `Q4_K` and `Q6_K`, you ***need*** to use `llama-quantize` on the model files produced by `convert_hf_to_gguf.py --outtype tq1_0` (and also for `tq2_0`). Otherwise these two tensors are kept as `f16` and are responsible for most of the size of the models.

```console
$ python3 convert_hf_to_gguf.py /path/to/TriLM_3.9B_Unpacked/ --outfile /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf --outtype tq1_0
$ ./build/bin/llama-quantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf tq1_0
```

If you want to try `TQ2_0`, which is faster (but bigger) than `TQ1_0` on compute-bound hardware, you can replace `tq1_0` with `tq2_0` in the above example, but it&#39;s also possible to convert from the `TQ1_0` model file.

The two ternary formats hold the same values, so round-trip quantizing between the two should result in the same files.

```console
$ ./build/bin/llama-quantize --allow-requantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ2_0.gguf tq2_0
```

## Speed

`TQ2_0` is twice as fast as `Q4_K` on my laptop. It&#39;s *the* fastest quant on compute-bound AVX2-capable computers.

This is a table of the float32-equivalent throughput of the `vec_dot_q` operation for each of these quant types.

| CPU | F16 | Q8_0 | Q4_K | Q2_K | TQ1_0 | TQ2_0 |
|-----|-----|------|------|------|-------|-------|
| Intel Core m3-8100Y (AVX2) | 30.60 GB/s | 67.03 GB/s | 64.17 GB/s | 81.73 GB/s | 70.31 GB/s | 141.83 GB/s |
| Arm Cortex A72 (NEON) | 3.84 GB/s | 9.51 GB/s| 9.26 GB/s | 9.79 GB/s | 11.81 GB/s | 15.78 GB/s |
| Arm Cortex A53 (NEON)| 4.30 GB/s | 5.87 GB/s | 5.76 GB/s | 5.84 GB/s | 8.97 GB/s | 10.29 GB/s |
| AWS `t4g` (NEON) | 8.69 GB/s | 22.35 GB/s | 25.34 GB/s | 22.84 GB/s | 33.34 GB/s | 44.80 GB/s |
| AWS `t4g` (DOTPROD) | 49.17 GB/s | 42.63 GB/s | 45.40 GB/s | 29.84 GB/s | 40.44 GB/s | 65.76 GB/s |

From this, it&#39;s easy to see that `TQ1_0` is usually slightly faster than `Q4_K`, and that `TQ2_0` is by far the fastest quant on AVX2.

&gt; [!NOTE]
&gt; There might be a way to make a similar type as `TQ2_0` like some sort of `Q2_1`, which could be almost as fast but still usable by non-ternary models, but this will probably *require* something like [LQER](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/discussions/8831) to help with keeping some precision.

## Size

The token embeddings are kept at `Q4_K` and the output projection at `Q6_K`, which means the smaller models might be slightly bigger than 2 bits per weight.

All of the [TriLM models](https://huggingface.co/collections/SpectraSuite/trilms-unpacked-668d5f62afe0f4036925b1d2) should work, because their row sizes are multiples of 256. I did not try them all yet, but those I tried are in the table below.

The BitNet b1.58 models [from the 1bitLLM team](https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM) however are *not* all compatible; only the 700M model has dimensions divisible by 256. The others are not supported (yet), unless when padding them.

| Model | F16 | TQ1_0 | TQ2_0 |
|-|-|-|-|
| &lt;https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM/bitnet_b1_58-large&gt; (728.84 M) | 1391.26 MiB | 176.65 MiB | 207.03 MiB |
| &lt;https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked&gt; | 750.39 MiB | 128.04 MiB | 140.98 MiB |
| &lt;https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_1.5B_Unpacked&gt; | 2892.09 MiB | 401.54 MiB | 460.04 MiB |
| &lt;https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_2.4B_Unpacked&gt; | 4696.86 MiB | 603.59 MiB | 703.26 MiB |
| &lt;https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_3.9B_Unpacked&gt; | 7616.43 MiB | 948.16 MiB | 1112.70 MiB |

&gt; [!NOTE]
&gt; The [1.3B BitNet b1.58 model](https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM/bitnet_b1_58-xl) has a FFN size of 5460 which factors into `2 2 3 5 7 13`, which is not convenient for ***any*** block-wise types based on powers of 2, so these tensors are kept as `F16`. My hypothesis is that 5460 was a typo for 5440 (factors into `2 2 2 2 2 2 5 17`), but it was kept for some reason, and *reproduced* by the 1bitLLM team. ***If anyone training ternary models reads this, PLEASE DON&#39;T USE `5460` FOR THE FFN SIZE!*** Please use multiples of 256 for your row sizes.

## Perplexity

Quality seems good. I don&#39;t have a powerful machine, so my tests only include the first 16 chunks of wikitext-2-raw with &lt;https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked&gt;.

The tests below use `Q4_K` token embeddings and `Q6_K` output tensor for `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0`, while `F16` token embeddings and output tensor is used in `TQ1_0_L` and `TQ2_0_L`.

|       | chunk | PPL | ln(PPL(Q)/PPL(base)) | KL Divergence | Δp RMS | Same top p |
|-------|--------------|-----|-------|-------|---------|---------|
| TQ1_0 | 16    | 23.6336 ± 1.0765 | 0.00463 ± 0.00141 | 0.00187 ± 0.00002 |    0.860 ± 0.020 % | 97.279 ± 0.255 % |
| TQ2_0 | 16    | 23.6336 ± 1.0765 | 0.00463 ± 0.00141 | 0.00187 ± 0.00002 | 0.860 ± 0.020 % | 97.279 ±  0.255 % |
| TQ1_0_L | 16  | 23.5758 ± 1.0746 | 0.00218 ± 0.00112 | 0.00034 ± 0.00001 | 0.405 ± 0.012 % | 98.971 ±  0.158 % |
| TQ2_0_L |  16 | 23.5758 ± 1.0746 | 0.00218 ± 0.00112 | 0.00034 ± 0.00001 | 0.405 ± 0.012 % | 98.971 ± 0.158 % |

From this it seems like there is no significant quality loss for the ternary quants for TriLM models (I think the difference with pure `f16` comes from the 8-bit activations), and that `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0` are completely equivalent in quality (and they should be, because lossless conversion between the two is possible).

## Structure of `TQ1_0`

This type relies on the fact that `3^5 == 243 &lt; 256 == 2^8`.

In a block of 256 elements, there are 240 elements encoded in 5 elements per byte, while the last 16 elements are encoded in 4 elements per byte.

This means `(240 / 5) + (16 / 4) == 48 + 4 == 52` bytes are used to pack 256 ternary weights (this is `1.625` bits per weight).

But there is also one `float16` scale per block, so the size of a block is 54 bytes making it a `1.6875 bpw` type. Even though it&#39;s not ideal, this is still `1.6875 / (log(3) / log(2)) ≈ 94%` of the best ternary packing efficiency.

In the table below I&#39;m describing the order of the elements within the bytes. I&#39;m using ranges to make this shorter, with the notation `start..end` where the start is inclusive and the end is exclusive. (So `0..3` is `{0, 1, 2}`)

Read this as if the ranges of a row are zipped together. A byte never contains more than 5 ternary values.

The ternary values are stored unsigned, so `{-1, 0, 1}` is stored as `{0, 1, 2}`.

| byte | x * 3&lt;sup&gt;-1&lt;/sup&gt; | x * 3&lt;sup&gt;-2&lt;/sup&gt; | x * 3&lt;sup&gt;-3&lt;/sup&gt; | x * 3&lt;sup&gt;-4&lt;/sup&gt; | x * 3&lt;sup&gt;-5&lt;/sup&gt; |
|-|-|-|-|-|-|
|`0..32`|`0..32`|`32..64`|`64..96`|`96..128`|`128..160`|
|`32..48`|`160..176`|`176..192`|`192..208`|`208..224`|`224..240`|
|`48..52`|`240..244`|`244..248`|`248..252`|`252..256`|N/A|

And then byte `52` and `53` contain the `float16` scale in little-endian.

Values are stored in fixed point to allow extracting the most significant digit first. This is explained in &lt;https://compilade.net/blog/ternary-packing&gt;.

## Structure of `TQ2_0`

This type was originally inspired by the `Q2_2` type made by @Eddie-Wang1120, but the block size, the order, and the mapping of the values are different.

`TQ2_0` started as an experiment to see how fast a 2-bit type can be compared to a 1.6-bit type on compute-bound hardware.

This packs each ternary value in 2 bits, which means each byte contains 4 values.

The ternary values are stored unsigned, so `{-1, 0, 1}` is stored as `{0, 1, 2}`.

Again, the ranges use the `start..end` notation where the start is inclusive and the end is exclusive, and the ranges of a row should be read as being zipped together (they advance in parallel in lockstep).

| byte | x &lt;&lt; 6 | x &lt;&lt; 4 | x &lt;&lt; 2 | x &lt;&lt; 0 |
|---|-|-|-|-|
|`0..32`|`96..128`|`64..96`|`32..64`|`0..32`|
|`32..64`|`224..256`|`192..224`|`160..192`|`128..160`|

And then byte `64` and `65` contain the `float16` scale in little-endian.

# TODO

- [x] Implement Numpy (de)quantization for `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0`
  - Done in &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151/commits/3a0bf17d5737508fc28f8c1ad1efb206160a7770&gt;
- [x] Allow `convert_hf_to_gguf.py` to directly convert a ternary model to a ternary encoding
  - Using `f16` for the token embeddings and output tensor because `Q4_K` and `Q6_K` quantization is not yet supported by `gguf-py`. This means `llama-quantize` *needs* to be used to quantize these tensors.
  - [ ] Make it more obvious that the models should go through `llama-quantize` afterwards.
    - Maybe use other type names, like `TQ1_0_L` or something?
- [x] Decide whether the `float16` scale should be before or after the packed weights
  - I&#39;d prefer it after because I feel like the scales are read after the weights in dot products, but the convention with the other types (except for `Q2_K`, `Q3_K` and `Q6_K`) is to keep the scale before.
  - Okay, I&#39;ve decided the scales should stay at the end.
- [x] More graceful fallback conversion with `llama-quantize`
  - Using `Q4_0` as a fallback type, because the smallest symmetric quant type is `Q8_0` but it&#39;s a bit big, so `Q4_0` it is (even though it&#39;s not ideal). Only relevant when row sizes are not multiples of 256.
- [x] Unify the `__ARM_FEATURE_DOTPROD` variants of the dot products of `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0` with their bare `__ARM_NEON` variants to reduce code duplication.
- [x] Test `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0` for correctness on an ARM CPU which supports dot product instructions
  - Tested on an AWS `t4g.small` instance.
  - [x] Also test relative performance for fun
- [ ] Should `TQ1_0`&#39;s first 48 bytes be divided in 3 sub-blocks of 16 bytes (80 elements) instead of one of 32 bytes (140 elements) and one of 16 bytes?
  - I&#39;ve done the 32-16 split to use 256-bit registers on AVX2 for the pow3 shifts for at least the 32 byte part, but 16-16-16 would be more regular, although it would require using 128-bit registers for all the ternary shifts. Not sure if there&#39;s a performance difference.
- [x] Rename references to &quot;BitNet 1.58b&quot; to &quot;BitNet b1.58&quot;. The &quot;b&quot; comes before in the paper.
  - &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151/commits/bfd2f21fb43525a8757a8c9e44032fd14bac222b&gt; 
- [x] Find a naming convention for BitNet quants and rename `Q1_3` and `Q2_2`
  - They were renamed and redesigned as `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0`. 
- [x] Decide to keep or to remove the optimization for `ggml_mul` when the broadcasted tensor only has a single element
  - The decision is to keep it. Ref: https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151#discussion_r1667699770 
- [x] Fix Android CI build issues.
  - It was apparently a problem with Arm 32-bit. Fixed in &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151/commits/8fbd59308b54729a191dcf3aee3388abfa7dd6e3&gt;

        <div class="edit-comment-hide">
  
  <task-lists disabled sortable>
    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block">
      <p dir="auto">This adds <code class="notranslate">1.6875 bpw</code> and <code class="notranslate">2.0625 bpw</code> quant types for TriLMs and BitNet b1.58 models. For now, these are named <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code>, respectively.<br>
I had given glimpses of this idea starting from <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" data-error-text="Failed to load title" data-id="2352416340" data-permission-text="Title is private" data-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/7931" data-hovercard-type="pull_request" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/7931/hovercard?comment_id=1640265346&amp;comment_type=review_comment" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/7931#discussion_r1640265346">#7931 (comment)</a>.</p>
<p dir="auto">The <code class="notranslate">1.6875 bpw</code> type mostly relies on the fact that <code class="notranslate">3<sup>5</sup> == 243 &lt; 256 == 2<sup>8</sup></code> to pack 5 trits per byte.</p>
<p dir="auto">(I also made <a href="https://compilade.net/blog/ternary-packing" rel="nofollow">a blog post about ternary packing</a> in an attempt to explain the core idea a bit more (storing the values in fixed-point to extract the most significant digit first with multiplications))</p>
<p dir="auto">Huge thanks to <a class="user-mention notranslate" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/Eddie-Wang1120/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="https://github.com/Eddie-Wang1120">@Eddie-Wang1120</a>, who motivated this by adding initial BitNet b1.58 support in <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" data-error-text="Failed to load title" data-id="2352416340" data-permission-text="Title is private" data-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/7931" data-hovercard-type="pull_request" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/7931/hovercard" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/7931">#7931</a>.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">How to try it</h2>
<p dir="auto">Using <a href="https://huggingface.co/collections/SpectraSuite/trilms-unpacked-668d5f62afe0f4036925b1d2" rel="nofollow">TriLM models</a> is the easiest because all of their models have row sizes divisible by 256.</p>
<div class="markdown-alert markdown-alert-important" dir="auto"><p class="markdown-alert-title" dir="auto"><svg data-component="Octicon" class="octicon octicon-report mr-2" viewBox="0 0 16 16" version="1.1" width="16" height="16" aria-hidden="true"><path d="M0 1.75C0 .784.784 0 1.75 0h12.5C15.216 0 16 .784 16 1.75v9.5A1.75 1.75 0 0 1 14.25 13H8.06l-2.573 2.573A1.458 1.458 0 0 1 3 14.543V13H1.75A1.75 1.75 0 0 1 0 11.25Zm1.75-.25a.25.25 0 0 0-.25.25v9.5c0 .138.112.25.25.25h2a.75.75 0 0 1 .75.75v2.19l2.72-2.72a.749.749 0 0 1 .53-.22h6.5a.25.25 0 0 0 .25-.25v-9.5a.25.25 0 0 0-.25-.25Zm7 2.25v2.5a.75.75 0 0 1-1.5 0v-2.5a.75.75 0 0 1 1.5 0ZM9 9a1 1 0 1 1-2 0 1 1 0 0 1 2 0Z"></path></svg>Important</p><p dir="auto">To quantize the token embeddings and the output tensor to <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code> and <code class="notranslate">Q6_K</code>, you <em><strong>need</strong></em> to use <code class="notranslate">llama-quantize</code> on the model files produced by <code class="notranslate">convert_hf_to_gguf.py --outtype tq1_0</code> (and also for <code class="notranslate">tq2_0</code>). Otherwise these two tensors are kept as <code class="notranslate">f16</code> and are responsible for most of the size of the models.</p>
</div>
<div class="highlight highlight-text-shell-session notranslate position-relative overflow-auto" dir="auto" data-snippet-clipboard-copy-content="$ python3 convert_hf_to_gguf.py /path/to/TriLM_3.9B_Unpacked/ --outfile /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf --outtype tq1_0
$ ./build/bin/llama-quantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf tq1_0"><pre class="notranslate">$ <span class="pl-s1">python3 convert_hf_to_gguf.py /path/to/TriLM_3.9B_Unpacked/ --outfile /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf --outtype tq1_0</span>
$ <span class="pl-s1">./build/bin/llama-quantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0-big.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf tq1_0</span></pre></div>
<p dir="auto">If you want to try <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code>, which is faster (but bigger) than <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> on compute-bound hardware, you can replace <code class="notranslate">tq1_0</code> with <code class="notranslate">tq2_0</code> in the above example, but it's also possible to convert from the <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> model file.</p>
<p dir="auto">The two ternary formats hold the same values, so round-trip quantizing between the two should result in the same files.</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-text-shell-session notranslate position-relative overflow-auto" dir="auto" data-snippet-clipboard-copy-content="$ ./build/bin/llama-quantize --allow-requantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ2_0.gguf tq2_0"><pre class="notranslate">$ <span class="pl-s1">./build/bin/llama-quantize --allow-requantize /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ1_0.gguf /somewhere/TriLM-3.9B-TQ2_0.gguf tq2_0</span></pre></div>
<h2 dir="auto">Speed</h2>
<p dir="auto"><code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> is twice as fast as <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code> on my laptop. It's <em>the</em> fastest quant on compute-bound AVX2-capable computers.</p>
<p dir="auto">This is a table of the float32-equivalent throughput of the <code class="notranslate">vec_dot_q</code> operation for each of these quant types.</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>CPU</th>
<th>F16</th>
<th>Q8_0</th>
<th>Q4_K</th>
<th>Q2_K</th>
<th>TQ1_0</th>
<th>TQ2_0</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Intel Core m3-8100Y (AVX2)</td>
<td>30.60 GB/s</td>
<td>67.03 GB/s</td>
<td>64.17 GB/s</td>
<td>81.73 GB/s</td>
<td>70.31 GB/s</td>
<td>141.83 GB/s</td>
</tr>

<td>3.84 GB/s</td>
<td>9.51 GB/s</td>

<td>10.29 GB/s</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>8.69 GB/s</td>
<td>22.35 GB/s</td>

</tr>

<td>42.63 GB/s</td>

<p dir="auto">From this, it's easy to see that <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> is usually slightly faster than <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code>, and that <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> is by far the fastest quant on AVX2.</p>
<div class="markdown-alert markdown-alert-note" dir="auto"><p class="markdown-alert-title" dir="auto"><svg data-component="Octicon" class="octicon octicon-info mr-2" viewBox="0 0 16 16" version="1.1" width="16" height="16" aria-hidden="true"><path d="M0 8a8 8 0 1 1 16 0A8 8 0 0 1 0 8Zm8-6.5a6.5 6.5 0 1 0 0 13 6.5 6.5 0 0 0 0-13ZM6.5 7.75A.75.75 0 0 1 7.25 7h1a.75.75 0 0 1 .75.75v2.75h.25a.75.75 0 0 1 0 1.5h-2a.75.75 0 0 1 0-1.5h.25v-2h-.25a.75.75 0 0 1-.75-.75ZM8 6a1 1 0 1 1 0-2 1 1 0 0 1 0 2Z"></path></svg>Note</p><p dir="auto">There might be a way to make a similar type as <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> like some sort of <code class="notranslate">Q2_1</code>, which could be almost as fast but still usable by non-ternary models, but this will probably <em>require</em> something like <a href="https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/discussions/8831" data-hovercard-type="discussion" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/discussions/8831/hovercard">LQER</a> to help with keeping some precision.</p>
</div>

<h2 dir="auto">Size</h2>
<p dir="auto">The token embeddings are kept at <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code> and the output projection at <code class="notranslate">Q6_K</code>, which means the smaller models might be slightly bigger than 2 bits per weight.</p>
<p dir="auto">All of the <a href="https://huggingface.co/collections/SpectraSuite/trilms-unpacked-668d5f62afe0f4036925b1d2" rel="nofollow">TriLM models</a> should work, because their row sizes are multiples of 256. I did not try them all yet, but those I tried are in the table below.</p>
<p dir="auto">The BitNet b1.58 models <a href="https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM" rel="nofollow">from the 1bitLLM team</a> however are <em>not</em> all compatible; only the 700M model has dimensions divisible by 256. The others are not supported (yet), unless when padding them.</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Model</th>
<th>F16</th>
<th>TQ1_0</th>
<th>TQ2_0</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><a href="https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM/bitnet_b1_58-large" rel="nofollow">https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM/bitnet_b1_58-large</a> (728.84 M)</td>
<td>1391.26 MiB</td>
<td>176.65 MiB</td>
<td>207.03 MiB</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked" rel="nofollow">https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked</a></td>
<td>750.39 MiB</td>
<td>128.04 MiB</td>
<td>140.98 MiB</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_1.5B_Unpacked" rel="nofollow">https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_1.5B_Unpacked</a></td>
<td>2892.09 MiB</td>
<td>401.54 MiB</td>
<td>460.04 MiB</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_2.4B_Unpacked" rel="nofollow">https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_2.4B_Unpacked</a></td>
<td>4696.86 MiB</td>

</tr>

<td>7616.43 MiB</td>

</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<div class="markdown-alert markdown-alert-note" dir="auto"><p class="markdown-alert-title" dir="auto"><svg data-component="Octicon" class="octicon octicon-info mr-2" viewBox="0 0 16 16" version="1.1" width="16" height="16" aria-hidden="true"><path d="M0 8a8 8 0 1 1 16 0A8 8 0 0 1 0 8Zm8-6.5a6.5 6.5 0 1 0 0 13 6.5 6.5 0 0 0 0-13ZM6.5 7.75A.75.75 0 0 1 7.25 7h1a.75.75 0 0 1 .75.75v2.75h.25a.75.75 0 0 1 0 1.5h-2a.75.75 0 0 1 0-1.5h.25v-2h-.25a.75.75 0 0 1-.75-.75ZM8 6a1 1 0 1 1 0-2 1 1 0 0 1 0 2Z"></path></svg>Note</p><p dir="auto">The <a href="https://huggingface.co/1bitLLM/bitnet_b1_58-xl" rel="nofollow">1.3B BitNet b1.58 model</a> has a FFN size of 5460 which factors into <code class="notranslate">2 2 3 5 7 13</code>, which is not convenient for <em><strong>any</strong></em> block-wise types based on powers of 2, so these tensors are kept as <code class="notranslate">F16</code>. My hypothesis is that 5460 was a typo for 5440 (factors into <code class="notranslate">2 2 2 2 2 2 5 17</code>), but it was kept for some reason, and <em>reproduced</em> by the 1bitLLM team. <em><strong>If anyone training ternary models reads this, PLEASE DON'T USE <code class="notranslate">5460</code> FOR THE FFN SIZE!</strong></em> Please use multiples of 256 for your row sizes.</p>
</div>
<h2 dir="auto">Perplexity</h2>
<p dir="auto">Quality seems good. I don't have a powerful machine, so my tests only include the first 16 chunks of wikitext-2-raw with <a href="https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked" rel="nofollow">https://huggingface.co/SpectraSuite/TriLM_390M_Unpacked</a>.</p>
<p dir="auto">The tests below use <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code> token embeddings and <code class="notranslate">Q6_K</code> output tensor for <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code>, while <code class="notranslate">F16</code> token embeddings and output tensor is used in <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0_L</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0_L</code>.</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th></th>
<th>chunk</th>
<th>PPL</th>

<th>KL Divergence</th>
<th>Δp RMS</th>
<th>Same top p</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>TQ1_0</td>
<td>16</td>
<td>23.6336 ± 1.0765</td>
<td>0.00463 ± 0.00141</td>
<td>0.00187 ± 0.00002</td>
<td>0.860 ± 0.020 %</td>
<td>97.279 ± 0.255 %</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>TQ2_0</td>
<td>16</td>
<td>23.6336 ± 1.0765</td>
<td>0.00463 ± 0.00141</td>
<td>0.00187 ± 0.00002</td>
<td>0.860 ± 0.020 %</td>
<td>97.279 ±  0.255 %</td>
</tr>

<td>16</td>

<td>0.00218 ± 0.00112</td>
<td>0.00034 ± 0.00001</td>
<td>0.405 ± 0.012 %</td>
<td>98.971 ±  0.158 %</td>

<td>16</td>
<td>23.5758 ± 1.0746</td>
<td>0.00218 ± 0.00112</td>
<td>0.00034 ± 0.00001</td>
<td>0.405 ± 0.012 %</td>
<td>98.971 ± 0.158 %</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<p dir="auto">From this it seems like there is no significant quality loss for the ternary quants for TriLM models (I think the difference with pure <code class="notranslate">f16</code> comes from the 8-bit activations), and that <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> are completely equivalent in quality (and they should be, because lossless conversion between the two is possible).</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Structure of <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code></h2>
<p dir="auto">This type relies on the fact that <code class="notranslate">3^5 == 243 &lt; 256 == 2^8</code>.</p>
<p dir="auto">In a block of 256 elements, there are 240 elements encoded in 5 elements per byte, while the last 16 elements are encoded in 4 elements per byte.</p>
<p dir="auto">This means <code class="notranslate">(240 / 5) + (16 / 4) == 48 + 4 == 52</code> bytes are used to pack 256 ternary weights (this is <code class="notranslate">1.625</code> bits per weight).</p>
<p dir="auto">But there is also one <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale per block, so the size of a block is 54 bytes making it a <code class="notranslate">1.6875 bpw</code> type. Even though it's not ideal, this is still <code class="notranslate">1.6875 / (log(3) / log(2)) ≈ 94%</code> of the best ternary packing efficiency.</p>
<p dir="auto">In the table below I'm describing the order of the elements within the bytes. I'm using ranges to make this shorter, with the notation <code class="notranslate">start..end</code> where the start is inclusive and the end is exclusive. (So <code class="notranslate">0..3</code> is <code class="notranslate">{0, 1, 2}</code>)</p>
<p dir="auto">Read this as if the ranges of a row are zipped together. A byte never contains more than 5 ternary values.</p>
<p dir="auto">The ternary values are stored unsigned, so <code class="notranslate">{-1, 0, 1}</code> is stored as <code class="notranslate">{0, 1, 2}</code>.</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>byte</th>
<th>x * 3<sup>-1</sup></th>
<th>x * 3<sup>-2</sup></th>
<th>x * 3<sup>-3</sup></th>
<th>x * 3<sup>-4</sup></th>
<th>x * 3<sup>-5</sup></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">0..32</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">0..32</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">32..64</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">64..96</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">96..128</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">128..160</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">32..48</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">160..176</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">176..192</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">192..208</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">208..224</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">224..240</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">48..52</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">240..244</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">244..248</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">248..252</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">252..256</code></td>
<td>N/A</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<p dir="auto">And then byte <code class="notranslate">52</code> and <code class="notranslate">53</code> contain the <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale in little-endian.</p>
<p dir="auto">Values are stored in fixed point to allow extracting the most significant digit first. This is explained in <a href="https://compilade.net/blog/ternary-packing" rel="nofollow">https://compilade.net/blog/ternary-packing</a>.</p>
<h2 dir="auto">Structure of <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code></h2>
<p dir="auto">This type was originally inspired by the <code class="notranslate">Q2_2</code> type made by <a class="user-mention notranslate" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/Eddie-Wang1120/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="https://github.com/Eddie-Wang1120">@Eddie-Wang1120</a>, but the block size, the order, and the mapping of the values are different.</p>
<p dir="auto"><code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> started as an experiment to see how fast a 2-bit type can be compared to a 1.6-bit type on compute-bound hardware.</p>
<p dir="auto">This packs each ternary value in 2 bits, which means each byte contains 4 values.</p>
<p dir="auto">The ternary values are stored unsigned, so <code class="notranslate">{-1, 0, 1}</code> is stored as <code class="notranslate">{0, 1, 2}</code>.</p>
<p dir="auto">Again, the ranges use the <code class="notranslate">start..end</code> notation where the start is inclusive and the end is exclusive, and the ranges of a row should be read as being zipped together (they advance in parallel in lockstep).</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>byte</th>
<th>x &lt;&lt; 6</th>
<th>x &lt;&lt; 4</th>
<th>x &lt;&lt; 2</th>
<th>x &lt;&lt; 0</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">0..32</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">96..128</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">64..96</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">32..64</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">0..32</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">32..64</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">224..256</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">192..224</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">160..192</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">128..160</code></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<p dir="auto">And then byte <code class="notranslate">64</code> and <code class="notranslate">65</code> contain the <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale in little-endian.</p>

<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Allow <code class="notranslate">convert_hf_to_gguf.py</code> to directly convert a ternary model to a ternary encoding
<ul class="contains-task-list">
<li>Using <code class="notranslate">f16</code> for the token embeddings and output tensor because <code class="notranslate">Q4_K</code> and <code class="notranslate">Q6_K</code> quantization is not yet supported by <code class="notranslate">gguf-py</code>. This means <code class="notranslate">llama-quantize</code> <em>needs</em> to be used to quantize these tensors.</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Incomplete task"> Make it more obvious that the models should go through <code class="notranslate">llama-quantize</code> afterwards.
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Maybe use other type names, like <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0_L</code> or something?</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Decide whether the <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale should be before or after the packed weights
<ul dir="auto">
<li>I'd prefer it after because I feel like the scales are read after the weights in dot products, but the convention with the other types (except for <code class="notranslate">Q2_K</code>, <code class="notranslate">Q3_K</code> and <code class="notranslate">Q6_K</code>) is to keep the scale before.</li>
<li>Okay, I've decided the scales should stay at the end.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> More graceful fallback conversion with <code class="notranslate">llama-quantize</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Using <code class="notranslate">Q4_0</code> as a fallback type, because the smallest symmetric quant type is <code class="notranslate">Q8_0</code> but it's a bit big, so <code class="notranslate">Q4_0</code> it is (even though it's not ideal). Only relevant when row sizes are not multiples of 256.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Unify the <code class="notranslate">__ARM_FEATURE_DOTPROD</code> variants of the dot products of <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> with their bare <code class="notranslate">__ARM_NEON</code> variants to reduce code duplication.</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Test <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> for correctness on an ARM CPU which supports dot product instructions
<ul class="contains-task-list">
<li>Tested on an AWS <code class="notranslate">t4g.small</code> instance.</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Also test relative performance for fun</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Incomplete task"> Should <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code>'s first 48 bytes be divided in 3 sub-blocks of 16 bytes (80 elements) instead of one of 32 bytes (140 elements) and one of 16 bytes?
<ul dir="auto">
<li>I've done the 32-16 split to use 256-bit registers on AVX2 for the pow3 shifts for at least the 32 byte part, but 16-16-16 would be more regular, although it would require using 128-bit registers for all the ternary shifts. Not sure if there's a performance difference.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li class="task-list-item"><input type="checkbox" id="" disabled="" class="task-list-item-checkbox" aria-label="Completed task" checked=""> Rename references to "BitNet 1.58b" to "BitNet b1.58". The "b" comes before in the paper.

</span>            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="Regarding the names of the new quant types, since these are quite specific to BitNet models, I was thinking to name them with something starting with `QB`, a bit like suggested in &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/issues/5761#issuecomment-2071270724&gt;.

I&#39;ll first be describing what I want from the naming scheme, then I&#39;ll attempt to make it work.

The naming scheme should have room for:
- Ternary types in `{-1, 0, 1}`
  - `1.625 bpw` quant with a block size of 64, with 13 bytes per block
    - To make the smallest possible lossless BitNet b1.58 model files
    - Uses `Q8_0` as its `vec_dot_type` (for the activations)
    - (It&#39;s technically possible to store a `float16` scale in the leftover bits in the last byte of 16 consecutive blocks (this means 1024 elements minimum per row), although it can&#39;t really be extracted with SIMD)
  - `2.000 bpw` quant with a block size of 32, with 8 bytes per block
    - For maximal performance
    - Uses `Q8_0` as its `vec_dot_type` (for the activations)
  - `2.000 bpw` quant with a block size of 64, with 16 bytes per block, and a float16 scale
    - Values would be packed similarly to the `1.625 bpw` type, but with an extra byte and a row-wise `float16` scale duplicated in each block.
  - `2.000 bpw` quant with a block size of 4, with 1 byte per block
    - For weirdly-shaped models like the 1.3B BitNet b1.58 model
    - Needs a compatible `vec_dot_type`
      - float types are slower than integer types for this
- Binary types in `{-1, 1}`
  - `1 bpw` type
- Binary types in `{0, 1}`
  - Are there models which use this?
- 8-bit activation with a row-wise scale
  - `8.5 bpw` like `Q8_0`, but all the scales of a row are the same
    - Would allow reducing the number of `float32` operations in the vec_dot of the above types.
  - `10 bpw`, 5 bytes per block of 4 elements, with a weird layout which only uses blocks to get a big enough buffer, with a single float32 scale and some padding before all row elements, aligned and contiguous.
    - For use with the weird `2.000 bpw` type, and also maybe the other ones for best performance.

So the naming scheme could be:

- `QB&lt;x&gt;_&lt;y&gt;`
  - where `&lt;x&gt;` is the floor of the expected bpw of the type
  - where `&lt;y&gt;` is
    - `0` binary type, `{0, 1}`
      - except for `QB8_0` which is like `Q8_0` but with a guaranteed duplicated row-wise scale
    - `1` binary type, `{-1, 1}`
    - `2` ternary type using some kind of binary-coded ternary
    - `3` ternary type with fixed-point packed values
    - `4` weird type with a block size of 4

Which for the previously-mentioned possible BitNet types would mean:

| proposed name | Range | bits per weight | block size | bytes | row-wise scale | current name |
|-|-|-|-|-|-|-|
|`QB1_3`| `{-1, 0, 1}` | 1.625 | 64 | 13 | 1.0f | `Q1_3` |
|`QB2_2`| `{-2, -1, 0, 1}` | 2.000 | 32 | 8 | 1.0f | `Q2_2` |
|`QB2_3`| `{-1, 0, 1}` | 2.000 | 64 | 16 | `f16` | |
|`QB2_4`| `{-2, -1, 0, 1}` | 2.000 | 4 | 1 | 1.0f | |
|`QB1_1`| `{-1, 1}` | 1.000 | ? | ?/8 | 1.0f | |
|`QB1_0`| `{0, 1}` | 1.000 | ? | ?/8 | 1.0f | |
|`QB8_0`| `[-127, 127]` | 8.5 | 32 | 34 | `f16` | |
|`QB8_4`| `[-127, 127]` | 10 | 4 | 5 | `f32`, weird layout | |

I&#39;m not saying these should all exist, though, only that the naming scheme should not be too limiting for possible future extensions (which might not exist anyway due to lack of time).

So I think I&#39;ll rename `Q1_3` to `QB1_3`, and `Q2_2` to `QB2_2`. Anyone has comments on this? Or a better naming scheme for the new BitNet quant types?" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
              Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>
    </div>

    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block css-overflow-wrap-anywhere">
      <p dir="auto">Regarding the names of the new quant types, since these are quite specific to BitNet models, I was thinking to name them with something starting with <code class="notranslate">QB</code>, a bit like suggested in <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" data-error-text="Failed to load title" data-id="2158525558" data-permission-text="Title is private" data-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/5761" data-hovercard-type="issue" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/5761/hovercard?comment_id=2071270724&amp;comment_type=issue_comment" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/5761#issuecomment-2071270724">#5761 (comment)</a>.</p>
<p dir="auto">I'll first be describing what I want from the naming scheme, then I'll attempt to make it work.</p>
<p dir="auto">The naming scheme should have room for:</p>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Ternary types in <code class="notranslate">{-1, 0, 1}</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li><code class="notranslate">1.625 bpw</code> quant with a block size of 64, with 13 bytes per block
<ul dir="auto">
<li>To make the smallest possible lossless BitNet b1.58 model files</li>
<li>Uses <code class="notranslate">Q8_0</code> as its <code class="notranslate">vec_dot_type</code> (for the activations)</li>
<li>(It's technically possible to store a <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale in the leftover bits in the last byte of 16 consecutive blocks (this means 1024 elements minimum per row), although it can't really be extracted with SIMD)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">2.000 bpw</code> quant with a block size of 32, with 8 bytes per block
<ul dir="auto">
<li>For maximal performance</li>
<li>Uses <code class="notranslate">Q8_0</code> as its <code class="notranslate">vec_dot_type</code> (for the activations)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">2.000 bpw</code> quant with a block size of 64, with 16 bytes per block, and a float16 scale
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Values would be packed similarly to the <code class="notranslate">1.625 bpw</code> type, but with an extra byte and a row-wise <code class="notranslate">float16</code> scale duplicated in each block.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">2.000 bpw</code> quant with a block size of 4, with 1 byte per block
<ul dir="auto">
<li>For weirdly-shaped models like the 1.3B BitNet b1.58 model</li>
<li>Needs a compatible <code class="notranslate">vec_dot_type</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>float types are slower than integer types for this</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Binary types in <code class="notranslate">{-1, 1}</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li><code class="notranslate">1 bpw</code> type</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Binary types in <code class="notranslate">{0, 1}</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Are there models which use this?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>8-bit activation with a row-wise scale
<ul dir="auto">
<li><code class="notranslate">8.5 bpw</code> like <code class="notranslate">Q8_0</code>, but all the scales of a row are the same
<ul dir="auto">
<li>Would allow reducing the number of <code class="notranslate">float32</code> operations in the vec_dot of the above types.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">10 bpw</code>, 5 bytes per block of 4 elements, with a weird layout which only uses blocks to get a big enough buffer, with a single float32 scale and some padding before all row elements, aligned and contiguous.
<ul dir="auto">
<li>For use with the weird <code class="notranslate">2.000 bpw</code> type, and also maybe the other ones for best performance.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">So the naming scheme could be:</p>
<ul dir="auto">
<li><code class="notranslate">QB&lt;x&gt;_&lt;y&gt;</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>where <code class="notranslate">&lt;x&gt;</code> is the floor of the expected bpw of the type</li>
<li>where <code class="notranslate">&lt;y&gt;</code> is
<ul dir="auto">
<li><code class="notranslate">0</code> binary type, <code class="notranslate">{0, 1}</code>
<ul dir="auto">
<li>except for <code class="notranslate">QB8_0</code> which is like <code class="notranslate">Q8_0</code> but with a guaranteed duplicated row-wise scale</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">1</code> binary type, <code class="notranslate">{-1, 1}</code></li>
<li><code class="notranslate">2</code> ternary type using some kind of binary-coded ternary</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">3</code> ternary type with fixed-point packed values</li>
<li><code class="notranslate">4</code> weird type with a block size of 4</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p dir="auto">Which for the previously-mentioned possible BitNet types would mean:</p>
<markdown-accessiblity-table><table role="table">
<thead>
<tr>
<th>proposed name</th>
<th>Range</th>
<th>bits per weight</th>
<th>block size</th>
<th>bytes</th>
<th>row-wise scale</th>
<th>current name</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB1_3</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{-1, 0, 1}</code></td>
<td>1.625</td>
<td>64</td>
<td>13</td>
<td>1.0f</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">Q1_3</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB2_2</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{-2, -1, 0, 1}</code></td>
<td>2.000</td>
<td>32</td>
<td>8</td>
<td>1.0f</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">Q2_2</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB2_3</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{-1, 0, 1}</code></td>
<td>2.000</td>
<td>64</td>
<td>16</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">f16</code></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB2_4</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{-2, -1, 0, 1}</code></td>
<td>2.000</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>1.0f</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB1_1</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{-1, 1}</code></td>
<td>1.000</td>
<td>?</td>
<td>?/8</td>
<td>1.0f</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB1_0</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">{0, 1}</code></td>
<td>1.000</td>
<td>?</td>
<td>?/8</td>
<td>1.0f</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB8_0</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">[-127, 127]</code></td>
<td>8.5</td>
<td>32</td>
<td>34</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">f16</code></td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code class="notranslate">QB8_4</code></td>
<td><code class="notranslate">[-127, 127]</code></td>
<td>10</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>5</td>
<td><code class="notranslate">f32</code>, weird layout</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></markdown-accessiblity-table>
<p dir="auto">I'm not saying these should all exist, though, only that the naming scheme should not be too limiting for possible future extensions (which might not exist anyway due to lack of time).</p>
<p dir="auto">So I think I'll rename <code class="notranslate">Q1_3</code> to <code class="notranslate">QB1_3</code>, and <code class="notranslate">Q2_2</code> to <code class="notranslate">QB2_2</code>. Anyone has comments on this? Or a better naming scheme for the new BitNet quant types?</p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  

</div>

    </div>

  <task-lists disabled sortable>
    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block css-overflow-wrap-anywhere">
      <p dir="auto">If it were me, considering this only works with bitnet models and nothing else, I'd want the designations to be exceptionally clear that they are different and shouldn't be used on just anything.  "QB" is good, but I'd take it a step further and remove the Q entirely.  As bitnet is being colloquially referred to as a "1-bit" model, B1 makes more sense.  Considering the plausible range for weights, I'd cut it off at tenths and ditch the decimal.  This leaves plenty of room for variations, while making the native BPW very clear.  I feel this is superior to the arbitrary "_2" and "_3" subtypes.</p>
<p dir="auto">So what I would propose is:</p>
<p dir="auto">1.625bpw = <code class="notranslate">B1_16</code><br>
2.000bpw = <code class="notranslate">B1_20</code></p>
    </div>
  </task-lists>
  

</div>

    </div>

| bitnet 3B Q2_2 - 2.000 bpw for BitNet b1.58 | 873.65 MiB |     3.32 B | BLAS       |      12 |         tg128 |     36.86 ± 0.12 |

What surprises me a little, after reading about `q2_2` being faster, is that `q1_3` seems to be faster with the setup I used here. Will investigate further.

| model                          |       size |     params | backend    | threads |          test |              t/s |
| ------------------------------ | ---------: | ---------: | ---------- | ------: | ------------: | ---------------: |
| bitnet 3B Q1_3 - 1.625 bpw for BitNet 1.58b | 729.64 MiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         pp512 |     15.15 ± 0.07 |
| bitnet 3B Q1_3 - 1.625 bpw for BitNet 1.58b | 729.64 MiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         tg128 |      9.87 ± 0.65 |
| bitnet 3B Q2_2 - 2.000 bpw for BitNet 1.58b | 873.65 MiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         pp512 |     19.25 ± 0.44 |
| bitnet 3B Q2_2 - 2.000 bpw for BitNet 1.58b | 873.65 MiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         tg128 |     13.07 ± 0.28 |
| bitnet 3B Q4_0                 |   1.79 GiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         pp512 |     18.44 ± 0.40 |
| bitnet 3B Q4_0                 |   1.79 GiB |     3.32 B | CPU        |       4 |         tg128 |      5.87 ± 0.12 |

I wonder if Q2_2 could be made faster if we used a block size of say 256 like the K-quants so that we can handle more than 64 bits of Q2_2 quants in each dot product loop. Aside from that I can&#39;t find any further way to improve that AVX implementation, and while it&#39;s ironic that we&#39;re using a `madds` instruction there when BitNet technically doesn&#39;t require multiplication that looks like the fastest way to dot the activations and ternary weights." data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">

        data-body-version="771c5e0edf02f6a2fd847563f86dd008e3aa7ee2f647708c88caeb9f26fc2692">
      <div class="timeline-comment-header clearfix d-flex flex-row flex-items-start"  data-morpheus-enabled="false">

      </span>

      <strong>
            <a class="author Link--primary text-bold css-overflow-wrap-anywhere " show_full_name="false" data-hovercard-type="user" data-hovercard-url="/users/compilade/hovercard" data-octo-click="hovercard-link-click" data-octo-dimensions="link_type:self" href="/compilade">compilade</a>

        <a href="#issuecomment-2198443770" id="issuecomment-2198443770-permalink" class="Link--secondary js-timestamp"><relative-time datetime="2024-06-30T05:51:01Z" class="no-wrap">Jun 30, 2024</relative-time></a>



      
    </div>

  </h3>

  <div class="d-flex flex-row-reverse flex-items-center flex-shrink-0 tmp-pl-3">
    <div class="timeline-comment-actions flex-shrink-0 d-flex flex-items-center">
      <details class="details-overlay details-reset position-relative d-inline-block">
        <summary data-view-component="true" class="timeline-comment-action Link--secondary Button--link Button--medium Button">  <span class="Button-content">
    <span class="Button-label"><svg aria-label="Show options" role="img" data-component="Octicon" height="16" viewBox="0 0 16 16" version="1.1" width="16" data-view-component="true" class="octicon octicon-kebab-horizontal">
    <path d="M8 9a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3ZM1.5 9a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3Zm13 0a1.5 1.5 0 1 0 0-3 1.5 1.5 0 0 0 0 3Z"></path>

        <details-menu
          class="dropdown-menu dropdown-menu-sw show-more-popover color-fg-default"
          style="width:185px"
          src=""
          preload
          
        >
            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy aria-label="Copy link" for="issuecomment-2198443770-permalink" role="menuitem" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
              Copy link

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="&gt; I wonder if Q2_2 could be made faster if we used a block size of say 256 like the K-quants

Can&#39;t go with bigger blocks than 64 elements or else the 3B model won&#39;t be fully quantizable. (Its FFN size is 8640 (which factors into `2 2 2 2 2 2 3 3 3 5`))

Its current block size is 32, which is the same as its vec_dot_type, Q8_0.

What would also help with performance would be to somehow use an 8-bit vec_dot_type having a single float scale per row. Might be interesting to explore later, but `ggml` does not have row-wise quant types yet, although this could still be done with a block quant.

&gt; it&#39;s ironic that we&#39;re using a `madds` instruction

Yeah, with AVX2, there are no good widening addition instructions like on ARM NEON, so `_mm256_maddubs_epi16` is used for that.

Meanwhile, NEON doesn&#39;t have the equivalent of `_mm_sign_epi8`, so it needs to use multiplications or conditional masks, which are both slower than a dedicated instruction doing zeroing and sign flipping like in SSSE3." data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
              Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>
    </div>

    <div class="d-none d-sm-flex">
        

  <span aria-label="This user has been invited to collaborate on the llama.cpp repository." data-view-component="true" class="tooltipped tooltipped-n">
    <span data-view-component="true" class="Label ml-1 tmp-ml-1">Collaborator</span>
</span>

  <span data-view-component="true" class="Label ml-1 tmp-ml-1">Author</span>

  <tbody class="d-block">
    <tr class="d-block">
      <td class="d-block comment-body markdown-body  js-comment-body">
          <blockquote>
<p dir="auto">I wonder if Q2_2 could be made faster if we used a block size of say 256 like the K-quants</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">Can't go with bigger blocks than 64 elements or else the 3B model won't be fully quantizable. (Its FFN size is 8640 (which factors into <code class="notranslate">2 2 2 2 2 2 3 3 3 5</code>))</p>
<p dir="auto">Its current block size is 32, which is the same as its vec_dot_type, Q8_0.</p>
<p dir="auto">What would also help with performance would be to somehow use an 8-bit vec_dot_type having a single float scale per row. Might be interesting to explore later, but <code class="notranslate">ggml</code> does not have row-wise quant types yet, although this could still be done with a block quant.</p>
<blockquote>
<p dir="auto">it's ironic that we're using a <code class="notranslate">madds</code> instruction</p>
</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">Yeah, with AVX2, there are no good widening addition instructions like on ARM NEON, so <code class="notranslate">_mm256_maddubs_epi16</code> is used for that.</p>
<p dir="auto">Meanwhile, NEON doesn't have the equivalent of <code class="notranslate">_mm_sign_epi8</code>, so it needs to use multiplications or conditional masks, which are both slower than a dedicated instruction doing zeroing and sign flipping like in SSSE3.</p>

      </td>
    </tr>
  </tbody>
</table>
</task-lists>

</span>            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="As proposed, the type utilizes only 3 of the 4 possible values. I was thinking that the `Q2_2` type would work the same as `Q4_0`, but assumes `amax == 1.0f`:

```c
void quantize_row_q2_2_reference(const float * restrict x, block_q2_2 * restrict y, int64_t k) {
    static const int qk = QK2_2;

    assert(k % qk == 0);

    const int nb = k / qk;

    for (int i = 0; i &lt; nb; i++) {
        float amax = 0.0f; // absolute max
        float max  = 0.0f;

        for (int j = 0; j &lt; qk; j++) {
            const float v = x[i*qk + j];
            if (amax &lt; fabsf(v)) {
                amax = fabsf(v);
                max  = v;
            }
        }

        // assume amax = 1.0f
        max /= amax;

        const float d  = max / -2;
        const float id = d ? 1.0f/d : 0.0f;

        for (int j = 0; j &lt; qk/4; ++j) {
            const float x0 = x[i*qk + 0*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x1 = x[i*qk + 1*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x2 = x[i*qk + 2*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x3 = x[i*qk + 3*qk/4 + j]*id;

            const uint8_t xi0 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x0 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi1 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x1 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi2 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x2 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi3 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x3 + 2.5f));

            y[i].qs[j]  = xi0;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi1 &lt;&lt; 2;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi2 &lt;&lt; 4;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi3 &lt;&lt; 6;
        }
    }
}
```

(not tested, just pattern matching the existing `quantize_row_q4_0_reference()`)

Edit: just realized the above would not work. We have assume that `max == 1.0f`, not `amax`, so:

```c
const float max = 1.0f;
...
```" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">

    <div class="comment-body markdown-body js-comment-body soft-wrap user-select-contain d-block css-overflow-wrap-anywhere">
      <p dir="auto">As proposed, the type utilizes only 3 of the 4 possible values. I was thinking that the <code class="notranslate">Q2_2</code> type would work the same as <code class="notranslate">Q4_0</code>, but assumes <code class="notranslate">amax == 1.0f</code>:</p>
<div class="highlight highlight-source-c notranslate position-relative overflow-auto" dir="auto" data-snippet-clipboard-copy-content="void quantize_row_q2_2_reference(const float * restrict x, block_q2_2 * restrict y, int64_t k) {
    static const int qk = QK2_2;

    assert(k % qk == 0);

    const int nb = k / qk;

    for (int i = 0; i &lt; nb; i++) {
        float amax = 0.0f; // absolute max
        float max  = 0.0f;

        for (int j = 0; j &lt; qk; j++) {
            const float v = x[i*qk + j];
            if (amax &lt; fabsf(v)) {
                amax = fabsf(v);
                max  = v;
            }
        }

        // assume amax = 1.0f
        max /= amax;

        const float d  = max / -2;
        const float id = d ? 1.0f/d : 0.0f;

        for (int j = 0; j &lt; qk/4; ++j) {
            const float x0 = x[i*qk + 0*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x1 = x[i*qk + 1*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x2 = x[i*qk + 2*qk/4 + j]*id;
            const float x3 = x[i*qk + 3*qk/4 + j]*id;

            const uint8_t xi0 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x0 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi1 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x1 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi2 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x2 + 2.5f));
            const uint8_t xi3 = MIN(3, (int8_t)(x3 + 2.5f));

            y[i].qs[j]  = xi0;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi1 &lt;&lt; 2;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi2 &lt;&lt; 4;
            y[i].qs[j] |= xi3 &lt;&lt; 6;
        }
    }
}"><pre class="notranslate"><span class="pl-smi">void</span> <span class="pl-en">quantize_row_q2_2_reference</span>(<span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-c1">*</span> restrict <span class="pl-s1">x</span>, <span class="pl-smi">block_q2_2</span> <span class="pl-c1">*</span> restrict <span class="pl-s1">y</span>, <span class="pl-smi">int64_t</span> <span class="pl-s1">k</span>) {
    <span class="pl-k">static</span> <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">int</span> <span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">QK2_2</span>;

    <span class="pl-en">assert</span>(<span class="pl-s1">k</span> % <span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">==</span> <span class="pl-c1">0</span>);

    <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">int</span> <span class="pl-s1">nb</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">k</span> / <span class="pl-s1">qk</span>;

    <span class="pl-k">for</span> (<span class="pl-smi">int</span> <span class="pl-s1">i</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">0</span>; <span class="pl-s1">i</span> <span class="pl-c1">&lt;</span> <span class="pl-s1">nb</span>; <span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">++</span>) {
        <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">amax</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">0.0f</span>; <span class="pl-c">// absolute max</span>
        <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">max</span>  <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">0.0f</span>;

        <span class="pl-k">for</span> (<span class="pl-smi">int</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">0</span>; <span class="pl-s1">j</span> <span class="pl-c1">&lt;</span> <span class="pl-s1">qk</span>; <span class="pl-s1">j</span><span class="pl-c1">++</span>) {
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">v</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">x</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span>];
            <span class="pl-k">if</span> (<span class="pl-s1">amax</span> <span class="pl-c1">&lt;</span> <span class="pl-en">fabsf</span>(<span class="pl-s1">v</span>)) {
                <span class="pl-s1">amax</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-en">fabsf</span>(<span class="pl-s1">v</span>);
                <span class="pl-s1">max</span>  <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">v</span>;
            }
        }

        <span class="pl-c">// assume amax = 1.0f</span>
        <span class="pl-s1">max</span> /= <span class="pl-s1">amax</span>;

        <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">d</span>  <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">max</span> / <span class="pl-c1">-2</span>;
        <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">id</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">d</span> ? <span class="pl-c1">1.0f</span>/<span class="pl-s1">d</span> : <span class="pl-c1">0.0f</span>;

        <span class="pl-k">for</span> (<span class="pl-smi">int</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-c1">0</span>; <span class="pl-s1">j</span> <span class="pl-c1">&lt;</span> <span class="pl-s1">qk</span>/<span class="pl-c1">4</span>; <span class="pl-c1">++</span><span class="pl-s1">j</span>) {
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">x0</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">x</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">0</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span>/<span class="pl-c1">4</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span>]<span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">id</span>;
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">x1</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">x</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">1</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span>/<span class="pl-c1">4</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span>]<span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">id</span>;
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">x2</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">x</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">2</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span>/<span class="pl-c1">4</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span>]<span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">id</span>;
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">float</span> <span class="pl-s1">x3</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">x</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">3</span><span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">qk</span>/<span class="pl-c1">4</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-s1">j</span>]<span class="pl-c1">*</span><span class="pl-s1">id</span>;

            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">uint8_t</span> <span class="pl-s1">xi0</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-en">MIN</span>(<span class="pl-c1">3</span>, (<span class="pl-smi">int8_t</span>)(<span class="pl-s1">x0</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">2.5f</span>));
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">uint8_t</span> <span class="pl-s1">xi1</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-en">MIN</span>(<span class="pl-c1">3</span>, (<span class="pl-smi">int8_t</span>)(<span class="pl-s1">x1</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">2.5f</span>));
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">uint8_t</span> <span class="pl-s1">xi2</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-en">MIN</span>(<span class="pl-c1">3</span>, (<span class="pl-smi">int8_t</span>)(<span class="pl-s1">x2</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">2.5f</span>));
            <span class="pl-k">const</span> <span class="pl-smi">uint8_t</span> <span class="pl-s1">xi3</span> <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-en">MIN</span>(<span class="pl-c1">3</span>, (<span class="pl-smi">int8_t</span>)(<span class="pl-s1">x3</span> <span class="pl-c1">+</span> <span class="pl-c1">2.5f</span>));

            <span class="pl-s1">y</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span>].<span class="pl-c1">qs</span>[<span class="pl-s1">j</span>]  <span class="pl-c1">=</span> <span class="pl-s1">xi0</span>;
            <span class="pl-s1">y</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span>].<span class="pl-c1">qs</span>[<span class="pl-s1">j</span>] |= <span class="pl-s1">xi1</span> &lt;&lt; <span class="pl-c1">2</span>;
            <span class="pl-s1">y</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span>].<span class="pl-c1">qs</span>[<span class="pl-s1">j</span>] |= <span class="pl-s1">xi2</span> &lt;&lt; <span class="pl-c1">4</span>;
            <span class="pl-s1">y</span>[<span class="pl-s1">i</span>].<span class="pl-c1">qs</span>[<span class="pl-s1">j</span>] |= <span class="pl-s1">xi3</span> &lt;&lt; <span class="pl-c1">6</span>;
        }
    }
}</pre></div>
<p dir="auto">(not tested, just pattern matching the existing <code class="notranslate">quantize_row_q4_0_reference()</code>)</p>

            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy aria-label="Copy link" for="issuecomment-2329861209-permalink" role="menuitem" data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>            <span data-view-component="true">
  <clipboard-copy role="menuitem" value="&gt; Is there any reason to not merge this?

Not really. It&#39;s pretty much ready (apart from support in other backends than CPU-only, and quantization to `Q4_K` and `Q6_K` not being supported in `gguf-py` yet, but I guess this can be fixed later once reference quantization is made platform independent (ref &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8939#issuecomment-2287784101&gt;)). I did have an hesitation with the order of the values in `TQ1_0`, but after making some experiments with indexing, its current structure (which ended up unchanged) *should* be good enough for future GPU implementations (hopefully).

&lt;details&gt;&lt;summary&gt;(the indexing experiment)&lt;/summary&gt;

Indices to extract 4 values per `tid`. This relies a lot on memory access coalescing, since each 5 consecutive `tid` will read the same 4 bytes (the last 4 bytes are only read by 4 `tid`, though).

This tests the read order.

```python3
tq1_0_pattern: list[list[int]] = [[32*v + b for v in range(5)] for b in range(32)] + [[16*v + b + 160 for v in range(5)] for b in range(16)] + [[4*v + b + 240 for v in range(4)] for b in range(4)]

print(f&quot;{tq1_0_pattern=}&quot;)

for tid in range(64):
    n = tid // 40;  # 0 or 1
    nh = tid // 60; # 0 or 1
    il = tid // 5;  # 0..13
    ir = tid % 5;   # 0..5
    l = 32 - 16*n - 12*nh; # 32, 16 or 4

    q: list[int] = [4*il + j for j in range(4)]
    y: list[int] = [128*n + 64*nh + 4*il + l*ir + j for j in range(4)];

    status = &quot;good&quot;
    for a, b in zip(q, y):
        if a &gt;= len(tq1_0_pattern) or b != tq1_0_pattern[a][ir]:
            status = &quot;bad&quot;
            break

    print(f&quot;{tid=}, {q=}, {y=}, {status=}&quot;)
```

&lt;/details&gt;

Support for other backends than CPU will be added in separate pull requests. The only non-CPU backends I can possibly implement (and test) with the hardware I have are Vulkan (and CUDA, but only in evenings and weekends). `TQ2_0` should be relatively easy to port everywhere, while `TQ1_0` will require more effort, but *should* still be implementable.

&gt; Don&#39;t let the perfect be the enemy of the good.

Right. And the recent commits (&lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151/commits/8d61607656c7624f598e976ed31dc6ada7f7596d&gt; and &lt;https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/8151/commits/75b3a09602d36838f69daeb0adab54797f878c02&gt;) should not be controversial (reducing the changes to `ggml_mul`, handling missing `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0` cases in `switch` statements for some `ggml` operators with a dequantization-based variant (`ggml_add`, `ggml_add1`, etc.), and adding some comments in `tests/test-backend-ops.cpp` about `TQ1_0` and `TQ2_0`).

I will merge this soon, either today or tomorrow if I forget." data-view-component="true" class="dropdown-item btn-link">
      
              Copy Markdown

</clipboard-copy>  <div aria-live="polite" aria-atomic="true" class="sr-only" data-clipboard-copy-feedback></div>
</span>        </details-menu>
      </details>
    </div>

</blockquote>
<p dir="auto">Right. And the recent commits (<a class="commit-link" data-hovercard-type="commit" data-hovercard-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/commit/8d61607656c7624f598e976ed31dc6ada7f7596d/hovercard" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/commit/8d61607656c7624f598e976ed31dc6ada7f7596d"><tt>8d61607</tt></a> and <a class="commit-link" data-hovercard-type="commit" data-hovercard-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/commit/75b3a09602d36838f69daeb0adab54797f878c02/hovercard" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/commit/75b3a09602d36838f69daeb0adab54797f878c02"><tt>75b3a09</tt></a>) should not be controversial (reducing the changes to <code class="notranslate">ggml_mul</code>, handling missing <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code> cases in <code class="notranslate">switch</code> statements for some <code class="notranslate">ggml</code> operators with a dequantization-based variant (<code class="notranslate">ggml_add</code>, <code class="notranslate">ggml_add1</code>, etc.), and adding some comments in <code class="notranslate">tests/test-backend-ops.cpp</code> about <code class="notranslate">TQ1_0</code> and <code class="notranslate">TQ2_0</code>).</p>
<p dir="auto">I will merge this soon, either today or tomorrow if I forget.</p>

      </td>
    </tr>

      <div class="pr-1 flex-auto min-width-0" >
        <code>
          <a title="ggml-quants : ternary packing for TriLMs and BitNet b1.58 (#8151)

* ggml-quants : 1.625 bpw ternary packing for BitNet 1.58b

* ggml-quants : faster 1.625 bpw AVX2 vec_dot

Not using a lookup table anymore makes it match q4_0 speed.

* gguf-py : fix formatting

* llama : remove spaces on empty line

* ggml-quants : subtract 1 when back in epi8

This makes the 1.625 bpw type go faster than q4_0. Still not the fastest.

* ggml-quants : Q2_2 now faster than Q4_K on with AVX2

* ggml-quants : cleanup Q1_3 code formatting

* ggml-quants : ARM NEON vec_dot for q2_2 and q1_3

* ggml-quants : use ceiling division when quantizing q1_3

* convert-hf : simplify BitNet pre-quantization

This still results in the exact same tensor weights and scales,
but it reveals some weirdness in the current algorithm.

* convert-hf : allow converting the weird BitNet 1.3B

Its FFN size is 5460 which is not convenient.
The offending tensors are kept in F16,
which makes the final model 5.01 bpw.

* bitnet : replace 1.58b with b1.58, as in the paper

* ggml-quants : fix build failure on Windows

* ggml-quants : attempt to fix Arm 32-bit support

* ggml : add some informative comments in q1_3 vec_dot

* ggml : add TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 ternary quantization types

* ggml : even faster TQ2_0

* ggml : also faster TQ1_0

Same optimization as for TQ2_0 by offsetting the sum instead of the weights.
This makes TQ1_0 almost as fast as Q8_0 on AVX2.

* ggml : fix build issues in certain environments

* ggml : add NEON vec_dot implementation for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

* ggml : avoid directly using vmlal_high_s8, for 32-bit ARM compat

The compiler seems smart enough to use the same instruction
even when using vget_high_s8 instead.

* ggml : remove q1_3 and q2_2

No more 1.625 bpw and 2.000 bpw,
now instead using 1.6875 bpw and 2.0625 bpw
with TQ1_0 and TQ2_0, respectively.

* llama : remove the separate scale tensors of BitNet b1.58

They won't be needed, since the remaining ternary quant types have
built-in scales.

* ggml-quants : rename fields of TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 structs for consistency

* ggml-quants : allow using vdotq_s32 in TQ2_0 vec_dot

Not yet tested on hardware which supports it,
might not work or might not even compile. But also it might.
It should make the performance better on recent ARM CPUs.

* ggml-quants : remove comment about possible format change of TQ2_0

Making it slightly more convenient for AVX512
but less convenient for everything else is not worth the trouble.

* gguf-py : Numpy (de)quantization for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

* ggml-quants : use roundf instead of nearest_int for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

This does not change anything for ternary models,
since their values should never end up being in halfway cases anyway.

* convert : allow direct conversion to TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

The token embeddings and output tensors are kept in F16
to allow quantizing them to Q4_K and Q6_K with llama-quantize.

* llama : handle fallback for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 with Q4_0

Q4_0 is not completely symmetric (so not lossless for ternary models),
but it should be good enough.

* ggml-quants : allow using ARM dot product instructions for TQ1_0

* ggml-quants : deduplicate TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 __ARM_FEATURE_DOTPROD support

* ggml : remove unused ggml_mul special case

It would otherwise conflict with the more general
optimization coming with Mamba-2.

* ggml : handle TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 in dequantization-based operators

* test-backend-ops : add TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 comments for later

Not yet adding uncommented, because some backends like SYCL and Metal
do not properly handle unknown types in supports_op for GGML_OP_MUL_MAT.
(and Metal also doesn't handle it with GGML_OP_GET_ROWS)
Support for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 for other backends than CPU
will be added in follow-up pull requests." data-pjax="true" class="Link--secondary markdown-title" href="/dsx1986/llama.cpp/commit/3ea4a8500ec9ec2d4d3730dcfe4c772278fb8fce">ggml-quants : ternary packing for TriLMs and BitNet b1.58 (</a><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" data-error-text="Failed to load title" data-id="2377250207" data-permission-text="Title is private" data-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/8151" data-hovercard-type="pull_request" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/8151/hovercard" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/8151">ggml-org#8151</a>
        </code>

          <span class="hidden-text-expander inline">
            <button type="button" class="ellipsis-expander js-details-target" aria-expanded="false" aria-label="Commit message body">&hellip;</button>
          </span>

      </div>

      <div class="pr-1 d-md-inline-block d-none">

        <code>
          <a href="/dsx1986/llama.cpp/commit/3ea4a8500ec9ec2d4d3730dcfe4c772278fb8fce" class="Link--secondary">3ea4a85</a>

    <div class="Details-content--hidden mt-2">
      <pre class="color-fg-muted ws-pre-wrap"><a class="issue-link js-issue-link" data-error-text="Failed to load title" data-id="2377250207" data-permission-text="Title is private" data-url="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/issues/8151" data-hovercard-type="pull_request" data-hovercard-url="/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/8151/hovercard" href="https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/pull/8151"></a>)

* ggml-quants : 1.625 bpw ternary packing for BitNet 1.58b

* ggml-quants : faster 1.625 bpw AVX2 vec_dot

Not using a lookup table anymore makes it match q4_0 speed.

* gguf-py : fix formatting

* llama : remove spaces on empty line

* ggml-quants : subtract 1 when back in epi8

This makes the 1.625 bpw type go faster than q4_0. Still not the fastest.

* ggml-quants : Q2_2 now faster than Q4_K on with AVX2

* ggml-quants : cleanup Q1_3 code formatting

* ggml-quants : ARM NEON vec_dot for q2_2 and q1_3

* ggml-quants : use ceiling division when quantizing q1_3

* convert-hf : simplify BitNet pre-quantization

This still results in the exact same tensor weights and scales,
but it reveals some weirdness in the current algorithm.

* convert-hf : allow converting the weird BitNet 1.3B

Its FFN size is 5460 which is not convenient.
The offending tensors are kept in F16,
which makes the final model 5.01 bpw.

* bitnet : replace 1.58b with b1.58, as in the paper

* ggml-quants : fix build failure on Windows

* ggml-quants : attempt to fix Arm 32-bit support

* ggml : add some informative comments in q1_3 vec_dot

* ggml : add TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 ternary quantization types

* ggml : even faster TQ2_0

* ggml : also faster TQ1_0

Same optimization as for TQ2_0 by offsetting the sum instead of the weights.
This makes TQ1_0 almost as fast as Q8_0 on AVX2.

* ggml : fix build issues in certain environments

* ggml : add NEON vec_dot implementation for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

* ggml : avoid directly using vmlal_high_s8, for 32-bit ARM compat

The compiler seems smart enough to use the same instruction
even when using vget_high_s8 instead.

* ggml : remove q1_3 and q2_2

No more 1.625 bpw and 2.000 bpw,
now instead using 1.6875 bpw and 2.0625 bpw
with TQ1_0 and TQ2_0, respectively.

* llama : remove the separate scale tensors of BitNet b1.58

They won't be needed, since the remaining ternary quant types have
built-in scales.

* ggml-quants : rename fields of TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 structs for consistency

* ggml-quants : allow using vdotq_s32 in TQ2_0 vec_dot

Not yet tested on hardware which supports it,
might not work or might not even compile. But also it might.
It should make the performance better on recent ARM CPUs.

* ggml-quants : remove comment about possible format change of TQ2_0

Making it slightly more convenient for AVX512
but less convenient for everything else is not worth the trouble.

* gguf-py : Numpy (de)quantization for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

* ggml-quants : use roundf instead of nearest_int for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

This does not change anything for ternary models,
since their values should never end up being in halfway cases anyway.

* convert : allow direct conversion to TQ1_0 and TQ2_0

The token embeddings and output tensors are kept in F16
to allow quantizing them to Q4_K and Q6_K with llama-quantize.

* llama : handle fallback for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 with Q4_0

Q4_0 is not completely symmetric (so not lossless for ternary models),
but it should be good enough.

* ggml-quants : allow using ARM dot product instructions for TQ1_0

* ggml-quants : deduplicate TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 __ARM_FEATURE_DOTPROD support

* ggml : remove unused ggml_mul special case

It would otherwise conflict with the more general
optimization coming with Mamba-2.

* ggml : handle TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 in dequantization-based operators

* test-backend-ops : add TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 comments for later

Not yet adding uncommented, because some backends like SYCL and Metal
do not properly handle unknown types in supports_op for GGML_OP_MUL_MAT.
(and Metal also doesn't handle it with GGML_OP_GET_ROWS)
Support for TQ1_0 and TQ2_0 for other backends than CPU
will be added in follow-up pull requests.</pre>
    </div>
</div>

    </div>
  </div>
</div>
